## Page 1


Stability of Synthetic Timelike Ricci Bounds under C0-Limits and
Applications to Impulsive Gravitational Waves
Andrea Mondino∗, Vanessa Ryborz†, and Clemens S¨amann‡
May 6, 2026
Abstract
We investigate the stability of timelike Ricci curvature lower bounds under low-regularity limits of
Lorentzian metrics. Specifically, we prove that the synthetic curvature-dimension condition TCDe
p(K, N),
which provides an optimal transport formulation of the Hawking–Penrose strong energy condition, is
stable under locally uniform convergence of smooth Lorentzian metrics, provided a uniform global hy-
perbolicity assumption holds. As a consequence, smooth locally uniform limits of vacuum spacetimes
satisfy the strong energy condition, even though curvature is not controlled a priori.
As a main application, we study impulsive gravitational waves — spacetimes with Lipschitz continuous
metrics — and show that large classes of such waves satisfy synthetic timelike Ricci curvature lower
bounds.
In the case of Minkowski background, we further establish synthetic upper Ricci curvature
bounds. Our approach relies on constructing suitable smooth approximations with lower bounds on the
timelike Ricci, and analyzing the limiting behavior via Lorentzian optimal transport.
These results yield new geometric insights into low-regularity solutions of the Einstein equations
and, in particular, provide a counterexample to the extension of the Eschenburg–Galloway–Newman
Lorentzian splitting theorem to infinitesimally Minkowskian TCDe
p(0, N) Lorentzian length spaces. More-
over, our construction shows that a direct Lorentzian analogue of the Cheeger–Colding almost splitting
theorem — under assumptions of almost non-negative timelike Ricci curvature and the existence of an
almost maximizing line — cannot hold. This highlights a fundamental difference between the geometry
of Riemannian and Lorentzian lower Ricci curvature bounds.
We also apply the aforementioned stability theorem to weak solutions of the Einstein equations arising
from the nonlinear interaction of impulsive gravitational waves.
Keywords: impulsive gravitational waves, Einstein equations, timelike Ricci curvature bounds, nons-
mooth spacetime geometry, general relativity, Lorentzian length spaces, low regularity, splitting theorem.
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 28A75, 51K10, 53C23, 53C50, 53B30, 53C80, 83C15, 83C35.

Introduction
Locally uniform limits of solutions to the vacuum Einstein equations satisfy the
Hawking–Penrose strong energy condition
Let (gj) be a sequence of smooth Lorentzian metrics defined on the smooth manifold M, solving the vacuum
Einstein equations
Ric(gj) ≡0,
(1.1)
and assume that gj →g locally uniformly, where g is a smooth Lorentzian metric. A basic problem is to
determine which geometric or analytic features of the sequence are stable under this limiting procedure.
Owing to the quasilinear, second-order nature of (1.1), the passage to the limit under mere C0 convergence
is highly nontrivial and, a priori, does not control curvature. Indeed, at a schematic level, one may write
Ric(g) = ∂2g + Q(∂g),
(1.2)
where Q(∂g) is a non-linear expression involving quadratic terms in the first order derivatives of the metric
g. In view of the non-linear term in (1.2), locally uniform limits of (gj) may retain nontrivial contributions
from quadratic expressions in ∂gj. From the perspective of nonlinear PDE, this places the problem within
the general paradigm in which lack of strong compactness gives rise to defect measures and effective source
terms; see, e.g., the compensated compactness theory of Tartar and Murat [71, 54] and its measure-valued
2


## Page 3


refinements [18]. In particular, there is no reason to expect stability of the vacuum condition under C0
convergence in the absence of additional structure.
A guiding example is provided by the high-frequency analysis of Einstein’s equations initiated by Isaacson
[32, 33]. There, rapidly oscillating families of vacuum solutions give rise, in an appropriate weak sense, to
an effective stress–energy tensor, leading formally to a limit equation of the form
Ric(g) = Teff,
(1.3)
where Teff is generated by quadratic oscillations of ∂gj. This phenomenon may be interpreted as a geometric
instance of compensated compactness for quasilinear wave equations.
Concrete realizations of (1.3) arise from families of vacuum plane waves. In suitable regimes, one obtains
limits satisfying
Ricµν(g) = 1
2α(u)2 ∂µu ∂νu,
(1.4)
corresponding to null dust stress–energy tensors of the form
Tµν = ρ kµkν,
kµkµ = 0,
ρ ≥0.
In particular, for any timelike vector X one has
Ric(X, X) = ρ(k · X)2 ≥0,
so that the Hawking–Penrose strong energy condition holds in these examples. More generally, such limits
are consistent with the expectation that effective matter arising from high-frequency gravitational fields
obeys natural positivity conditions.
A far-reaching conjectural framework was proposed by Burnett [8], asserting that suitable weak limits of high-
frequency vacuum solutions converge to solutions of the Einstein–massless Vlasov system. This essentially
means that high-frequency gravitational waves behave collectively like a gas of massless particles (photons
or gravitons). In this setting, the effective stress–energy tensor takes the form
Tµν(x) =
Z
pµpν f(x, p) dp,
f ≥0,
which automatically satisfies the strong energy condition.
The high frequency assumption in Burnett’s
conjecture can be stated precisely as follows:
1. C0-convergence:
There exists a C∞-Lorentzian metric g and a decreasing sequence (λj)j∈N with
limj→∞λj = 0, such that
|gj −g| ≤λj;
(1.5)
2. High frequency: There exists a constant C > 0 — possibly depending on M and on the sequence gj,
but independent of j — such that
|∂gj| ≤C,
|∂2gj| ≤λ−1
j .
(1.6)
This conjecture connects the problem of stability — under C0-convergence — of the vacuum Einstein equa-
tions to kinetic theory and suggests a robust mechanism enforcing positivity in the limit. Substantial progress
toward this conjecture has been obtained in recent years [31, 27, 29], for a survey see [30]
Parallel to these developments, there has been substantial progress on weak and low-regularity formulations
of the Einstein equations. The foundational work of Geroch and Traschen [22] identifies a maximal stable
class of metrics with distributional curvature, namely metrics whose first derivatives lie in L2
loc, thereby
providing a coordinate-invariant setting in which the Einstein tensor can be interpreted as a distribution.
3


## Page 4


Subsequent contributions (see for instance the work by LeFloch and Mardare [41]) develop weak formulations
and compactness results for C0 ∩W 1,2-Lorentzian metrics, yielding weak notions of the Einstein equations
compatible with geometric limits.
In a complementary direction, a substantial body of work has been devoted to the study of the Einstein
vacuum equations at low regularity. Building on earlier formulations in harmonic (wave) coordinates, Klain-
erman and Rodnianski [34] established local well-posedness for rough initial data in Sobolev spaces close to
the scaling-critical threshold, relying on a refined analysis of the null structure of the equations and bilinear
spacetime estimates. This line of research was further developed using microlocal and Fourier-analytic tech-
niques, notably by Smith and Tataru [66], who proved low-regularity well-posedness results for quasilinear
wave equations, including the Einstein equations in wave coordinates, at nearly optimal regularity. Subse-
quent works have refined these methods and extended the range of admissible data, combining paradifferential
calculus, Strichartz estimates, and geometric analysis. These results show that the Einstein equations admit
a robust well-posedness theory below classical smoothness, provided one retains quantitative control of first
derivatives in suitable Sobolev spaces. At the same time, these approaches remain fundamentally tied to
Sobolev regularity and do not extend to the C0 setting, where no intrinsic formulation of the equations is
currently available.
In conclusion, the aforementioned literature indicates that the passage to the limit in (1.1) is intimately
related to general principles of weak convergence and rigidity for nonlinear geometric PDEs. In particular, it
highlights the persistence of oscillation and concentration phenomena in curvature, when the metric converges
merely in C0. Beyond the high-frequency or the Sobolev regimes described above, the behavior of arbitrary
C0 limits of vacuum metrics remains largely open. In particular, there is currently no general mechanism –
at the PDE level – ensuring that positivity properties of curvature persist under C0-limits (1.5), dropping
the high frequency assumption (1.6).
This leads naturally to the question of whether intrinsic positivity properties of curvature can be recovered
directly from the vacuum structure of the approximating sequence, without additional assumptions. The
first main result of the present work answers this question affirmatively by showing that the limit Lorentzian
metric necessarily satisfies the Hawking–Penrose strong energy condition:
Ricg(X, X) ≥0
for all timelike vectors X.
(1.7)
Before stating the result, we introduce the following terminology.
Definition 1.1 (Uniform global hyperbolicity). A sequence of smooth Lorentzian metrics (gj)j∈N on M is
said to be uniformly globally hyperbolic, if for any two compact sets K1, K2 ⊂M,

We refer to Section 2.5 for a sufficient condition ensuring uniform global hyperbolicity, and to Appendix A

locally uniformly to a globally hyperbolic limit g∞, which fails to be uniformly globally hyperbolic.
We are now in a position to state the first main result.
Theorem 1.2. Let M be a smooth n-dimensional manifold and g be a smooth globally hyperbolic Lorentzian
metric. Let (gj)j∈N be a sequence of smooth Lorentzian metrics on M such that
1. gj →g locally uniformly;
2. (M, gj) are uniformly globally hyperbolic;
3. (M, gj) solve the Einstein vacuum equations with cosmological constant Λ ∈R, i.e., Ric(gj) ≡Λgj.

Ricg(X, X) ≥Λ |g(X, X)|
for all timelike vectors X.
(1.8)
Theorem 1.2 is a special case of Theorem 2.28, which establishes the stability of the synthetic, optimal-
transport formulation of the strong energy condition — namely the TCDe
p(K, N) and wTCDe
p(K, N) condi-
tions — under C0-convergence. Introduced in [12] as a Lorentzian counterpart of the Lott–Sturm–Villani
CD(K, N) condition [68, 69, 42], these synthetic curvature-dimension conditions are equivalent to the classical
ones in the smooth Lorentzian setting [45, 53]. At the same time, they remain stable under low-regularity
limits, thereby providing a robust framework for capturing curvature positivity beyond the smooth cate-
gory. We illustrate this point on a classical toy example. Let (fj) be a sequence of convex C2 functions
fj : [0, 1] →R converging uniformly to a function f : [0, 1] →R. The convexity of fj can be expressed either
in the differential form
f ′′

(1.9)
or in the synthetic form
fj(tx + (1 −t)y) ≤t fj(x) + (1 −t) fj(y)
for all t, x, y ∈[0, 1].
(1.10)
While the stability of (1.10) under C0 convergence is immediate, the corresponding statement for (1.9) is
not. One may view (1.9) as a toy model for the classical strong energy condition (1.8), and (1.10) as a toy
model for its synthetic, optimal-transport formulation TCDe
p(K, N), thereby illustrating why the latter is
better suited to low-regularity limits.
Penrose’s impulsive gravitational waves
Originally introduced by Penrose [56, 57], impulsive gravitational waves provide a model for highly energetic
yet extremely brief bursts of gravitational radiation.
These idealized models are remarkable both from
the physical and from the mathematical point-of-view: First, they consider a spacetime background of
constant curvature (flat or (anti-)de Sitter) and assume a source of gravitational radiation located at infinity
(or effectively “outside” the model). From this external source, a gravitational wave propagates through
the spacetime, localized on a null hypersurface. Off this null hypersurface the spacetime is smooth and
has constant curvature. Moreover, the passing of the wave gives rise to the so-called memory effect, see
[2, 15, 67]. From a mathematical point of view, impulsive gravitational waves are particularly significant
because they provide models of spacetimes endowed with Lorentzian metrics of low regularity; see [60]. In
one representation — the so-called Rosen form (3.1) — the metric is locally Lipschitz continuous. There
is, however, a physically equivalent Brinkmann form, in which the metric is distributional and explicitly
contains a Dirac delta term. The Brinkmann form has the advantage that, both before and after the impulse,
the metric manifestly coincides with that of the constant-curvature background. This can be vividly and
geometrically visualized via the so-called “cut-and-paste” method, cf. [26, Ch. 20]. These two forms are
related by a so-called “discontinuous coordinate transformation” [59]. Nevertheless, this equivalence can be
established in a mathematically rigorous way; see [64]. Various methods of constructing the different forms
of impulsive gravitational waves are reviewed in [58, 60].
So far, impulsive gravitational waves have been studied mathematically exclusively by analytical methods
— for example, through the use of Filippov geodesics and regularization techniques [58, 65]. In this work,
we approach them for the first time from a synthetic perspective, that is, by analyzing their geometry —
and in particular their curvature — within the framework of (Lorentzian) metric measure geometry, which
neither relies on a manifold structure nor on differential calculus. Such an approach proved to be extremely
fruitful, see [11, 63, 47, 4] for recent reviews on this topic. In particular, within the framework of Lorentzian
(pre-)length spaces [37], Cavalletti and the first author introduced synthetic timelike lower Ricci curvature
bounds [12]. Their construction extends to the non-smooth setting an optimal transport characterization of
timelike Ricci curvature bounds for smooth spacetimes, obtained independently by McCann [45] and by the
first author together with Suhr [53].
5


## Page 6


Impulsive gravitational waves are exact solutions to the vacuum Einstein equations (if the profile function
H satisfies ∆H = 0) as the Ricci curvature can be computed explicitly (even though the metric is singular
on a null hypersurface), see [26, Ch. 20] for more details.
In this work we establish for the first time
that impulsive gravitational waves have timelike Ricci curvature bounded from below, i.e., satisfy the TCD-
condition (if ∆H ≤0). Moreover, we also study timelike upper curvature bounds and the synthetic vacuum
Einstein equations [53].
We study impulsive gravitational waves in their (Lipschitz) continuous formulation, after first establishing
the C0-stability of the TCD-condition.
The stability of the TCD-condition under measured convergence
of the underlying Lorentzian length spaces — via Lorentzian isometric embeddings — was proved in [12,
Thm. 3.15]. Here, by contrast, we establish its stability with respect to C0-convergence, a result that is of
considerable independent interest. More precisely, we prove that the TCD-condition holds for continuous
Lorentzian metrics arising as C0-limits of suitable approximating sequences of smooth Lorentzian metrics.
We then apply this result to impulsive gravitational waves, for which we will construct explicit smooth
approximations.
The outlined strategy naturally splits the article into two parts.
The first part studies the stability of
synthetic timelike Ricci curvature bounds under the locally uniform convergence of Lorentzian metrics on
smooth manifolds. In the second part, these results are applied to prove synthetic timelike Ricci curvature
bounds for impulsive gravitational waves.
The main result of the first part is:
Theorem 2.28 (Stability of the TCD condition under C0-convergence of the metric). Let M be a smooth
n-dimensional manifold and g∞be a continuous Lorentzian metric on M that turns (M, g∞) into a globally
hyperbolic space such that every length-maximising causal curve has a causal character. Let (gj)j∈N be a
sequence of smooth Lorentzian metrics on M such that
1. gj →g∞locally uniformly;
2. (M, gj) are uniformly globally hyperbolic;
3. (M, gj) satisfy Ricgj(v, v) ≥K|gj(v, v)| for all v ∈TM timelike.
Then (M, g∞) satisfies the wTCDe
p(K, N)-condition, for all p ∈(0, 1) and N ≥n.
In particular, if the limit (in C0
loc-sense) metric g∞is smooth, then it satisfied the Hawking–Penrose strong
energy condition (with cosmological constant K ∈R): Ricg∞(v, v) ≥K|g∞(v, v)|, for all v ∈TM timelike.
To prove Theorem 2.28, we first analyse the behaviour of the causal relations ≪and ≤, as well as the time
separation function τ for the continuous limit of smooth Lorentzian metrics. This is achieved in Section 2.
A key step in our argument is to show that if a sequence of smooth Lorentzian metrics gj converges locally
uniformly to a continuous Lorentzian metric g∞, and if (gj)j∈N is uniformly globally hyperbolic (see Definition
1.1), then the associated time separation functions τj converge locally uniformly to τ∞as j →∞(see
Proposition 2.19). This result constitutes a crucial ingredient in the proof of Theorem 2.28, as well as in
the proof of a corresponding stability statement for synthetic timelike upper Ricci curvature bounds (see
Theorem 2.33).
Section 3 is devoted to the application of our main result to impulsive gravitational waves. The corresponding
Lorentzian metric gH,Λ is Lipschitz continuous; it depends on a profile function H: R4 →R, which encodes
the geometry of the propagating wave, and on the cosmological constant Λ ∈R, which characterizes the
constant-curvature background spacetime. For the explicit form of the metric, see (3.1) and (3.2).

We briefly outline the strategy of the proof.
The Lipschitz continuous Rosen form gH,Λ of the impulsive gravitational wave enables us to compute the
(measure-valued) distributional Ricci curvature tensor and to identify the regions in which it admits lower
or upper bounds. Exploiting this information, we construct smooth approximations gε such that gε →gH,Λ
locally uniformly and Ric[gε] is bounded from below. More precisely, the measure-valued Ricci curvature of
gH,Λ admits the decomposition into absolutely continuous and singular parts
Ric[gH,Λ] = Ric[gH,Λ](a) + Ric[gH,Λ](s).
Likewise, we will decompose the (now smooth) Ricci curvature of gε as
Ric[gε] = Ric[gε](a) + Ric[gε](s),
with Ric[gε](a) converging locally uniformly to Ric[gH,Λ](a) and Ric[gε](s) converging distributionally to
Ric[gH,Λ](s), while preserving its sign.
The construction proceeds by mollifying the non-smooth profile U + in the expression (3.1) of gH,Λ, and
analyzing the resulting curvature through explicit coordinate expressions. While most terms behave well
under this regularization, certain nonlinear interactions fail to converge uniformly and must be isolated. To
compensate for these defects, we introduce suitable correction terms in the metric, designed to cancel the
non-vanishing contributions arising from the mollification. This yields a refined approximation scheme in
which all problematic terms are either controlled or canceled, ensuring convergence of the Ricci tensor in the
above sense. For the details on the construction of the smooth approximations gε, see Section 3.2.
As a consequence, the approximating metrics gε fall within the scope of the stability Theorem 2.28, which
allows us to establish the wTCDe
p(Λ, 4) condition for a large class of impulsive gravitational waves on back-
grounds of non-negative constant curvature Λ ≥0, as well as a local version of the same condition for waves
propagating on an anti-de Sitter background. Since (R4, gH,Λ) is timelike non-branching, we conclude by
invoking [3, Thm. 3.35], which yields the equivalence of the wTCDe
p(Λ, 4) and TCDe
p(Λ, 4) conditions in this
setting.
Regarding recent developments related to Theorem 3.12, we highlight the work [5] by Braun and S´alamo
Candal, which shows that Lipschitz spacetimes with non-negative timelike Ricci curvature in a distributional
sense satisfy the TMCPp(0, N) condition. Recall from [12] that both the wTCDe
p(0, N) and TCDe
p(0, N)
conditions imply TMCPp(0, N).
Remark 1.3 (Failure of Lorentzian almost splitting and non-smooth splitting). In the recent work [61],
the second-named author proved that if (M, g) is a causally simple spacetime endowed with a continuous
Lorentzian metric g, then the associated Lorentzian length space is infinitesimally Minkowskian, in the sense
7


## Page 8


of [1]. Combining this result with Theorem 3.12, we infer that the impulsive gravitational wave (R4, gH,Λ)
with Λ = 0 — that is, with Minkowski background — satisfies the Lorentzian (entropic) timelike curvature-
dimension condition LTCDe
p(0, 4) for all p ∈(0, 1), introduced in [1].
An interesting geometric feature of the spacetime (R4, gH,0) is that all timelike geodesics are complete and
maximizing (see [40, 39]; see also Proposition 3.3). In particular, (R4, gH,0) contains a timelike line (see
Corollary 3.14).
Since (R4, gH,0) does not split isometrically along such a timelike line, it furnishes a counterexample to any
extension of the Eschenburg–Galloway–Newman Lorentzian splitting theorem [19, 21, 55], to LTCDe
p(0, N)
Lorentzian length spaces. We also note that this splitting theorem has recently been extended to Lorentzian
metrics of class C1, see [6]. As the metric gH,0 is Lipschitz continuous, this example furthermore shows
that the C1-regularity assumption cannot, in general, be weakened to C0,1. This is in sharp contrast with
positive signature, where the splitting theorem — first established in smooth Riemannian manifolds with non-
negative Ricci curvature by Cheeger–Gromoll [14] — was proved to hold in RCD(0, N) metric measure spaces
by Gigli [23]. Recall also that if (M, g) is an N-dimensional manifold with a Lipschitz Riemannian metric
with non-negative Ricci in distributional sense, then it is an RCD(0, N) space, see [51, 36].
A further consequence is the failure of a Lorentzian analogue of the Cheeger–Colding almost splitting theorem
[13]. Indeed, we observe that:
(a) The smooth approximations gε of gH,0 have timelike Ricci curvature bounded below by −δ(ε), with
δ(ε) →0 as ε →0.
(b) Let γ : R →R4 be a complete and maximizing geodesic for the metric gH,0. Since
gε →gH,0
locally uniformly,
it follows that γ is almost maximizing with respect to gε; that is, for every subinterval [a, b] ⊂R,
τgε(γa, γb) −Lgε(γ|[a,b]) →0
as ε →0,
where τgε and Lgε denote the time-separation function and the length functional associated with gε,
respectively.
In the Riemannian setting, the analogues of (a) and (b) — namely, almost non-negative Ricci curvature and
the existence of an almost line — imply, by the Cheeger–Colding almost splitting theorem, that the manifold
is close (in the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff sense) to one that splits isometrically off an R-factor.
By contrast, in the situation described above, the metrics gε converge to gH,0 in the C0
loc-topology (and hence
in any reasonable Lorentzian analogue of pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence; see, e.g., [50, 10, 52]), yet
the limit spacetime (R4, gH,0) does not admit an isometric splitting off an R-factor.
For impulsive gravitational waves on Minkowski background, we also show validity of synthetic upper bounds
on the Ricci curvature (in the sense of [53]). This is the content of the third main result stated below.
Theorem 3.16. If Λ = 0, the impulsive gravitational wave (R4, gH,Λ) with profile function H has synthetic
timelike Ricci curvature bounded from above by 0, provided that ∆H ≥c|D2H|, for some c > 0.
The proof of Theorem 3.17 proceeds by performing a suitable change of coordinates, which may be interpreted
as a Lorentz transformation adapted to impulsive gravitational waves, and by explicitly constructing optimal
transport plans that directly verify the synthetic upper timelike Ricci curvature bounds.
As a further application of the stability result established in Theorem 2.28, we show in Section 4 that the
weak solutions of the Einstein equations constructed by Luk and Rodnianski in [44] fit naturally into the
framework of TCDe
p(0, 4) Lorentzian length spaces. These weak solutions include, in particular, the nonlinear
interaction of weak impulsive gravitational waves on a flat background.
8


## Page 9


Finally, in the appendices, we present an example demonstrating that global hyperbolicity is strictly weaker
than uniform global hyperbolicity, and we provide additional technical details concerning the construction
of the approximations employed in Section 3.

2
Stability of synthetic curvature dimension conditions for con-
tinuous spacetimes
2.1
Lorentzian pre-length spaces and timelike curvature dimension conditions
In this section, we briefly recall some basic notions from the theory of Lorentzian pre-length spaces and
synthetic timelike lower Ricci curvature bounds thereof.
A Lorentzian pre-length space shall be seen as the Lorentzian analogue of a metric spaces (while Lorentzian
length spaces are the analogues of metric length spaces). These notions were introduced in [37] by follow-
ing earlier works of Busemann [9] and Kronheimer–Penrose [35]. At present, several variants of the basic
axiomatization of these spaces have been developed; see [46, 7, 1, 52]. We also mention related alternative
frameworks, such as bounded Lorentzian metric spaces [50, 10]. We have chosen to work within the original
framework of [37], since our setting is that of smooth manifolds, where any auxiliary Riemannian background
metric h induces the given manifold topology through its associated length metric dh.
Definition 2.1 ([37] Def. 2.8). A causal space is a set X endowed with a preorder ≤and a transitive relation
≪contained in ≤.
A Lorentzian pre-length space (X, d, ≪, ≤, τ) is a causal space (X, ≪, ≤) equipped with a proper metric d
(i.e., closed and bounded subsets are compact) and a lower semi-continuous function τ : X × X →[0, ∞],
called time separation function, that satisfies
τ(x, y) + τ(y, z) ≤τ(x, z), ∀x ≤y ≤z (reverse triangle inequality)
τ(x, y) > 0 ⇐⇒x ≪y, τ(x, y) = 0 if x ̸≤y.
(2.1)
We define the chronological (resp. causal) future of a subset A ⊂X as
I+(A) := {y ∈X : ∃x ∈A, x ≪y},
J+(A) := {y ∈X : ∃x ∈A, x ≤y},
respectively. Analogously we define the chronological and causal pasts I−(A), J−(A). In case A = {x}, we
write I±(x), J±(x) with a slight abuse of notation.
Definition 2.2. A non-constant curve γ : I →X is called (future-directed) timelike (resp. causal) if γ is
locally Lipschitz continuous with respect to d and for each s, t ∈I, s < t, it holds γs ≪γt, (resp. γs ≤γt).
We say that γ is a null curve if it is causal and no two points in γ(I) are related by ≪.

Definition 2.3. For γ : [a, b] →X future-directed causal, we set
Lτ(γ) := inf
( N

If I is half-open, say I = [a, b), we take the infimum over all partitions of the form a = t0 < t1 < · · · < tN < b;
the definition for other types of intervals is treated analogously.
Given a g-causal curve γ in a smooth Lorentzian spacetime, we a priori have that Lτ(γ) ≥Lg(γ). However,
if the spacetime is strongly causal, Proposition 2.32 in [37] shows that Lτ = Lg.
Definition 2.4. A causal curve γ is called a geodesic if it is maximal and continuous when parametrised
by τ-arclength, i.e., the set of causal geodesics is given by
Geo(X) := {γ ∈C([0, 1], X) : τ(γs, γt) = (t −s)τ(γ0, γ1)}.
The set of timelike geodesics is defined as follows:
TGeo(X) := {γ ∈Geo(X) : τ(γ0, γ1) > 0}.
A Lorentzian pre-length space (X, d, ≪, ≤, τ) is called
• non-totally imprisoning if for every compact set K ⊂X there exists a C > 0 such that every causal
curve contained in K has d-length at most C.
• globally hyperbolic if it is non-totally imprisoning and for every x, y ∈X, the causal diamond J+(x) ∩
J−(y) is compact.
• geodesic if for all x, y ∈X, x ≤y there exists a curve γ ∈Geo(X) connecting them.
By [37, Theorem 3.28], the time separation function τ in a globally hyperbolic Lorentzian geodesic space is
finite and continuous.
The next proposition is due to Minguzzi [49, Corollary 3.8].
Proposition 2.5. Let (X, d, ≪, ≤, τ) be a Lorentzian geodesic space. It is globally hyperbolic if and only if
for any two compact sets K1, K2 ⊂X, the causal diamond J+(K1) ∩J−(K2) is compact and the relation
{x ≤y} ⊂X2 is closed in X2.
Definition 2.6. A measured Lorentzian pre-length space (X, d, ≪, ≤, τ, m) is a Lorentzian pre-length space
(X, d, ≪, ≤, τ) endowed with a non-negative Radon measure m with supp m = X.

The next proposition is inspired by [62, Thm. 1.5 and Lem. 2.7].
Proposition 2.14. Let ˜gk be constructed in Proposition 2.13. Then there exist J0, k0 ∈N and a constant
C > 0 such that:
• Any ˜gk0-causal curve that is contained in K has h-length at most C;
• Any gj-causal curve γ contained in K has h-length at most C, for all j ≥J0.
Proof. Let ˜gk be as in Proposition 2.13. We will prove the proposition by contradiction. Assume that for all
k ≥1, we can find ˜gk-causal curves γk : [0, k] →K that are parameterised by h-arclength. As K is compact,
up to passing to a subsequence, we may assume that γk(0) →x ∈K as k →∞. Note that all the γk are
˜g1-causal. By [48, Thm. 3.1 (1)], there exists a ˜g1-causal curve γ : [0, ∞) →K such that γ(0) = x and (a
subsequence of) γk converges locally uniformly to γ. We will now prove that γ is g∞-causal. Since this is
a local property, we may restrict to some interval [a, b], where 0 ≤a < b < ∞. Let g′ be any continuous
metric that satisfies g∞≺g′. By [62, Lem. 1.4], there exists k′ ∈N such that ˜gk ≺g′ on K, for all k ≥k’.
Now (γk|[a,b])k≥k′ is a sequence of g′-causal curves, that has again a subsequence converging uniformly to
a g′-causal limit curve γ′ : [a, b] →K. By the uniqueness of limits, it follows that γ|[a,b] = γ′, hence γ is
g′-causal on [a, b]. This holds for any continuous g′ ≻g∞and all intervals [a, b] ⊂[0, ∞). By [17, Prop.
1.5], it follows that γ is g∞-causal. Hence, there exists a g∞-causal curve γ contained in K whose h-length
is infinite.
This contradicts [62, Lem. 2.7], since (M, g∞) was assumed to be globally hyperbolic.
This
concludes the proof of the first claim. In order to prove the second claim, set J0 := Jk0. We get that any
gj-causal curve contained in K is ˜gk0-causal, hence it has h-length at most C > 0.

Lemma 2.16. Let M be a smooth manifold and let K ⊂M be a compact subset. Then for each Borel
measure µ with supp µ ⊂K, there exists a countable basis for the w∗-open topology on P(K) containing µ.
Proof. Since every smooth manifold admits a Riemannian metric, and hence a corresponding Riemannian
distance function, it follows that M is metrizable and therefore so is K. A classical result from functional
analysis then implies that C(K) is separable. Moreover, it is well known that for any separable Banach
space X, the unit ball of the dual space, (BX∗, w∗), is metrizable with respect to the weak∗topology. In
particular, there exists a countable basis of w∗-open neighbourhoods of 0. Taking X = C(K) and recalling
that its dual space C(K)∗coincides with the space of finite regular Borel measures, the claim follows by
translation.
Lemma 2.17. Assume the metrics (gj)j∈N are smooth and uniformly globally hyperbolic and converge locally
uniformly to g∞, a continuous Lorentzian metric. Moreover, assume that any two points (x, y) ∈M 2 with
τ∞(x, y) > 0 can be joined by a timelike and Lipschitz continuous g∞-maximizer (when parametrized with
respect to arclength). Then the family (τj)j∈N is locally equicontinuous.

Proposition 2.19. Assume the metrics (gj)j∈N are smooth, uniformly globally hyperbolic and converge
locally uniformly to g∞, a continuous Lorentzian metric. Moreover, assume that any two points (x, y) ∈M 2
with τ∞(x, y) > 0 can be joined by a timelike and Lipschitz continuous g∞-maximizer (when parametrized
with respect to arclength). Then, there is a subsequence of (τj)j∈N that converges to τ∞locally uniformly. In
particular, τ∞is continuous.
Proof. Fix a compact set K ⊂M. Lemma 2.17 implies the equicontinuity of the family (τj)j∈N on K2. More-
over, the τj are uniformly bounded on K2. Thus, by Arzela–Ascoli’s Theorem, there exists a subsequence
of the τj that converges locally uniformly to a continuous function τ ′. We will next prove that τ∞= τ ′.
Step 1: τ∞≥τ ′. This was established in the first step of the proof of Lemma 2.17, cf. Remark 2.18 (i).
Step 2: τ∞≤τ ′. Assume the contrary: then there exist ε > 0 and x, y ∈K such that
τ∞(x, y) > τ ′(x, y) + ε ≥ε > 0.

In this subsection, we will use the previous results to prove the stability of the timelike curvature dimension
condition. This requires one more preparatory lemma.

The following result is inspired by [12, Thm. 1.5], where the stability of synthetic timelike Ricci lower
bounds was established under a Lorentzian version of pointed measured Gromov convergence (see [24] for
the corresponding metric framework), relying on isometric embeddings into a common Lorentzian pre-length
space. In contrast, our approach replaces the assumption of such isometric embeddings by requiring locally
uniform convergence of the Lorentzian metrics. This perspective will be crucial in the subsequent sections,
where we show that impulsive gravitational waves satisfy synthetic timelike Ricci lower bounds.
Theorem 2.28. Let M be a smooth manifold and g∞be a continuous globally hyperbolic Lorentzian metric
on M such that every length-maximising causal curve has a causal character. For all j ∈N, let gj be a
smooth Lorentzian metric on M satisfying the following assumptions:
• gj →g∞locally uniformly;
• (M, gj) are uniformly globally hyperbolic (see Definition 2.15);
• there exist p ∈(0, 1), N ∈(0, ∞) and K ∈R such that the measured Lorentzian length spaces induced
by (M, gj) satisfy the TCDe
p(K, N)-condition.
Then (M, g∞) satisfies the wTCDe
p(K, N)-condition.

We start this subsection by analysing stability properties of cyclical monotonicity. Then we recall synthetic
upper Ricci curvature bounds from [53] and prove its stability for locally uniformly converging Lorentzian
metrics gj →g∞under suitable uniform controls on gj.

The following result is inspired by [53, Thm. B.6], where the stability of synthetic timelike Ricci upper
bounds was established under a Lorentzian version of pointed measured Gromov convergence (see [24] for
the corresponding metric framework), relying on isometric embeddings into a common Lorentzian pre-length
space. In contrast, our approach replaces the assumption of such isometric embeddings by requiring locally
uniform convergence of the Lorentzian metrics. This perspective will be crucial in the subsequent sections,
where we show that impulsive gravitational waves satisfy synthetic timelike Ricci upper bounds.
Theorem 2.33. Let M be a smooth manifold endowed with a continuous Lorentzian metric g∞that turns
(M, g∞) into a globally hyperbolic geodesic space such that every length-maximising causal curve has a causal
character. For all j ∈N, let gj be smooth Lorentzian metrics on M such that (M, gj) are uniformly globally
hyperbolic (see Definition 2.15) and gj →g∞locally uniformly.

respect to p, r0 and remainder function ω.

2.5
A sufficient condition for uniform global hyperbolicity
Uniform global hyperbolicity plays a crucial role in Theorems 2.28 and 2.33. However, this assumption is
not automatic: there exist sequences of smooth globally hyperbolic Lorentzian metrics gj on Rd+1, d ≥2,
converging locally uniformly to a smooth globally hyperbolic limit g∞, which fail to be uniformly globally
hyperbolic (see Appendix A). In this subsection, we provide a sufficient condition ensuring uniform global
hyperbolicity.

3
Application to nonexpanding impulsive gravitational waves
The aim of this section is to show that Penrose’s impulsive gravitational waves fit into the framework of
measured Lorentzian geodesic spaces with synthetic lower and upper bounds on timelike Ricci curvature, as
stated in Theorems 3.12 and 3.16. We briefly outline the strategy.
In [58], Podolsk`y, Steinbauer, ˇSvarc, and the third author constructed nonexpanding impulsive gravitational
waves in constant curvature spacetimes. We focus on the Lipschitz continuous Rosen form of the metric (see
[58, Section 2.2]), for which we compute the distributional Ricci tensor and identify the conditions under
which it admits lower and/or upper bounds.
In the bounded case, we approximate the Lipschitz metric locally uniformly by smooth metrics with corre-
sponding curvature bounds. We then perform a further modification to ensure uniform global hyperbolicity,
thereby enabling the application of the results from the previous section. The construction of these smooth
approximations proceeds in several steps in order to maintain control of the Ricci curvature; we refer to the
beginning of Section 3.2 for an overview.

loc(C2). Let Λ ∈R

Proposition 3.3 (Impulsive gravitational waves are timelike non-branching). Every impulsive gravitational
wave in the continuous form (3.1) (with arbitrary cosmological constant Λ ∈R) is timelike non-branching.
Proof. By [39, Thm. 1.1] we know that every maximizer has a parametrization as a (Filippov) geodesic and in
that parametrization is C1,1. Moreover, we only need to consider timelike maximizers that cross the impulse
(otherwise we are in the smooth background spacetime of constant curvature, which is non-branching). By
[58, Thm. 3.3] all (causal) geodesics uniquely cross the impulse, hence are non-branching.

3.2
Smooth approximations of the continuous IPP-wave metric
The goal of this subsection is to construct smooth metrics gε that approximate locally uniformly the contin-
uous IPP-wave g as in (3.1), so that the Ricci curvature converges locally uniformly in the following sense.
Recall that by subsubsection 3.1.1, Ric[g] is a measure. Write Ric[g] = Ric[g]a+Ric[g]s, where Ric[g]a denotes
the absolutely continuous part with respect to the volume measure and Ric[g]s denotes the singular part.
We construct gε such that the Ricci curvature tensor can be decomposed into Ric[gε] = Ric[gε](a) +Ric[gε](s)
such that
(i) Ric[gε](a) →Ric[g]a locally uniformly (when seen as L∞-functions).
(ii) Ric[gε](s) →Ric[g]s distributionally.
(iii) If Ric[g]s is non-negative/non-positive, then for all ε > 0, Ric[gε](s) is non-negative/non-positive.
We quickly explain the strategy to achieve that. In a first step, we will naively replace every U + with a

3.3
Uniform global hyperbolicity of the smooth approximations
To prove that IPP-waves satisfy the TCD condition, we will apply the stability Theorem 2.28 to suitable
smooth approximations whose timelike Ricci curvature admits uniform lower bounds. A key assumption in
Theorem 2.28 is that the approximating metrics are uniformly globally hyperbolic (see Definition 2.15). The
purpose of this section is to modify the smooth approximations constructed in Propositions 3.6 and 3.7 so
that this condition is satisfied.

From the above discussion, we conclude that ˇgε →g locally uniformly, and that the Ricci curvature of ˇgε
converges to that of g both distributionally (globally), and locally uniformly away from {U = 0}. We will
henceforth restrict ˇgε to DomLor(ˇgε) ∩DomLor(g) ⊂R4.
We next recall some basic properties of Lorentzian spaces of constant curvature. Let m denote the Minkowski
metric on R4, i.e. R4 endowed with coordinates (t, w, x, y) and metric m = diag(−1, 1, 1, 1). For Λ ∈R, one
obtains a Lorentzian metric of constant sectional curvature Λ/3 (and hence constant Ricci curvature Λ) via
the conformal transformation
mΛ := m
P2

,
where

If Λ ̸= 0, the function PΛ may vanish; we denote its zero set by
NΛ := P−1
Λ (0),
and set OΛ := R4\NΛ. A direct computation shows that (OΛ, mΛ) has constant sectional curvature Λ/3, and
hence constant Ricci curvature Λ. Notice that if Λ > 0, NΛ consists of two distinct connected hypersurfaces
that separate OΛ into three connected components and for Λ < 0, NΛ consists of one connected hypersurface
that splits OΛ into two connected components.
The following lemma shows that if two points p, q ∈R4 have a compact causal diamond with respect to mΛ,
i.e. if J+

remains disjoint from {PΛ ≤0} and J+

4
Application to weak solutions of the Einstein equations and non-
linearly interacting impulsive gravitational waves
In [43], Luk and Rodnianski study the characteristic initial value problem for impulsive gravitational waves
and prove local existence results for the vacuum Einstein equations within certain classes of such waves,
where the impulse propagates along compact two-surfaces — in contrast to the classical impulsive waves
considered here. In the subsequent work [44], the same authors analyze the nonlinear interaction of impul-
sive gravitational waves, again in the framework of a characteristic initial value problem with initial data
prescribed on two null hypersurfaces. They establish the existence of weak solutions to the Einstein equa-
tions for a broad class of initial data posed on the null hypersurfaces H0 and H0. Moreover, they show that
smooth approximations γn of the initial data γ give rise to smooth solutions gn of the Einstein equations
that converge locally uniformly to the corresponding weak solution g.
We apply our stability result for the TCD-condition to the non-smooth spacetimes constructed in [43, 44],
thereby showing that a large class of non-smooth Lorentzian metrics — including models describing inter-
acting impulsive gravitational waves — satisfy the wTCD(0, 4)-condition.
Finally, we emphasize that the characteristic initial value problem is solved in the future of the impulse (or
of the interacting impulses). By contrast, our approach in the previous sections captures the entire impulsive
region and establishes the curvature bound Ric ≥0 across it – in a synthetic sense, see Theorem 3.12.
4.1
Setup
We restrict attention to the setting of [44], as it provides a more general framework than [43]. In this work,
the characteristic initial value problem is formulated with initial data prescribed on two null hypersurfaces
H0 and H0, intersecting along the sphere S0,0. The resulting spacetime is constructed as a solution to the
Einstein equations in a neighbourhood of H0 and H0 containing S0,0.
For a spacetime in a neighbourhood of S0,0, [44] defines a double null foliation as follows: consider solutions
u, u to the eikonal equation
(g−1)µ,ν∂µu∂νu = 0, (g−1)µ,ν∂µu∂νu = 0,

These are null geodesic vector fields. Let

Proof. From Theorem 4.1, we get that there exists an ε > 0 and a sequence of smooth metrics (M, gn)
defined on 0 ≤u ≤u∗, 0 ≤u ≤u∗, u∗≤ε such that they satisfy the vacuum Einstein equations and such
that gn →g locally in C0. Moreover, Theorem 4.1 gives us that the domain of existence equals the future of
the same hypersurfaces, hence Proposition 2.35 gives us uniform global hyperbolicity of the metrics (M, gn)
and (M, g). Now all conditions to apply Theorem 2.28 are met, which proves the proposition.

Locally uniform convergence does not imply uniform global hy-
perbolicity
Recall that uniform global hyperbolicity (see Definition 2.15) was a key assumption to obtain the stability
of lower bounds on the timelike Ricci curvature under C0
loc-convergence of Lorentzian metrics (see Theorem
1.2 and Theorem 2.28).
A natural question is whether such a notion is redundant. In order to show it is not, in this appendix we
construct metrics g, (gj)j∈N such that:
1. g and each gj is Lipschitz and globally hyperbolic;
2. gj converges to g locally uniformly;
3. the family (gj)j∈N is not uniformly globally hyperbolic.
It will be clear from the construction that one can regularise gj and g to smooth Lorentzian metrics still
satisfying the properties 1,2,3 above.

Claim 1. S = S1 ∪S2 is acausal.
Proof of claim 1.
Let p ̸= q ∈S and suppose that p < q.
The only case to consider is if p, q are in
different Sis (i = 1, 2). Then there exists a smooth g-causal curve γ connecting them, which we assume to be
parametrised by uniform t-speed, then it follows that t(p) < t(q). Note that projecting γ onto the subspace
{y = z = 0} yields a g-causal curve. Indeed, the time component of the velocity remains unchanged, while
the metric can only decrease in the spacelike directions (as ϕ is increasing), and only one of the three spatial
directions is retained. From now on, we work with this reduced curve. From the definition of S, it follows
